8. Ballaram S/o Mahajan Aged About 42 Years R/o Village Kanshitola, Police

82
Hon'ble Shri P. R. Ramachandra Menon, Chief Justice
Hon'ble Shri Justice Parth Prateem Sahu, Judge
CAV JUDGMENT
P. R. Ramachandra Menon, Chief Justice
1. The Petitioners, who are the elected office bearers of different Primary
Agricultural Credit Co-operative Societies in the different districts of the State of
Chhattisgarh are before this Court seeking to declare Clause-8 of the
Reorganization Scheme, 2019 (Annexure P/1) as unconstitutional, invalid,
arbitrary, beyond jurisdiction and to have it struck down accordingly.
2. Shri Prafull N. Bharat, learned counsel led the arguments on behalf of the
Petitioners and we heard Shri Amrito Das, learned Additional Advocate General
appearing for the State/Department.
3. The sum and substance of the contentions/submissions raised/made from the
part of the Petitioners is that the Respondents have virtually decided to take
over the management and administration of all the Primary Agricultural Credit
Co-operative Societies in the State without any regard to the tenure of the
elected bodies who came to power in a democratic exercise, that too; based on
executive instructions, with reference to the Scheme as aforesaid.
4. It is stated that as many as 1333 Primary Agricultural Credit Co-operative
Societies are there in the State. Even though, the report of the Registrar
referred to in Annexure P/1 Reorganization Scheme says that only “some” of
the Societies are decided to be reorganized, the exercise is sought to be
pursued throughout the State, adversely affecting almost all the similar Co-operative Societies.
5. The learned counsel for the Petitioners points out that, by virtue of Clause-8 of
Annexure P/1 Reorganization Scheme, right from the date of coming into
operation of the Scheme, the elected Board in the affected Societies shall
cease to work and in order to manage the business of the Society; any person

83
or persons may be appointed temporarily by the orders of the Sub/Assistant
Registrar for the period stated in the order or till the election of the new Board.
6. In Clause-5 of the Annexure P/1 Reorganization Scheme, the procedure of
reorganization is mentioned, where it is stated in sub-Clause (a) that copies of
the said plan shall be sent to the affected Societies; upon which they shall
submit their representation within 15 days; a member of that affected and
resultant Society may submit representation to the Sub/Assistant Registrar
within 15 days from the date of publication of the plan in the Gazette – sub-Clause (b); that the Sub/Assistant Registrar shall examine the representation
and submit his opinion to the Registrar, who in turn shall forward the same to
the Government along with his opinion within a further period of 15 days – sub-Clause (c); with the further stipulation under sub-Clause (d) that the
Government shall dispose of the representation within 30 days, making it clear
that it shall be final as mentioned under sub-Clause (e).
7. It is the case of the Petitioners that the said provision has been made otiose
since Clause 1(b) of Annexure P/1 Reorganization Scheme stipulates that the
plan shall come into force from the date of publication in the State Gazette,
which has already been effected on 28.08.2019 and as such, there is no 'pre-decisional hearing'. The Petitioners are sought to be ousted from office with
unwarranted haste, under the cover of reorganization. It is also stated that the
Petitioners are not against reorganization, but they are against the
undemocratic way in which, the elected bodies are sought be thrown out from
the office.
8. The Petitioners place reliance on various provisions of the Constitution of India
(as amended 97th amendment in the Constitution) and in particular, Article
243ZJ which stipulates the tenure of the Society to be 'five years' from the date
of election; adding that in the instant cases, they were elected quite recently
and their tenure is still to be completed. This Court is aware that the 97th

84
amendment to the Constitution has been set aside as unconstitutional by a
Division Bench of the Gujarat High Court, which however is pending
consideration before the Apex Court, though no interim stay has been granted.
But there is another contention for the Petitioners that tenure of the Societies is
clear from the statutory provision, particularly Section 49(7-A) of the
Chhattisgarh Co-operative Societies Act, 1960 (hereinafter referred to as 'the
Act'), which makes it 'five years'. It is also stated that no notice has been issued
to the Petitioners so far, as mentioned in Annexure P/1 Reorganization Scheme
inviting objections or to file representation, if any. The Scheme does not say
anything with regard to the contents of the report submitted by the Registrar or
as to the ways and means as to how it is to be achieved by the Government by
reorganization and there is a total vacuum in this regard, but for the eagerness
to oust the elected bodies and take over the management. The course of action
pursued is contrary to the mandate of Section 16 of the Act and in particular, as
to the right of the affected parties/members to exercise an 'option', whether to
continue to join the band or to get back their shares because of the change
sought to be brought in. It is also pointed out with reference to Annexure P/2
dated 27.08.2019 produced in Writ Petition (C) No.3075 of 2019, that
instructions have already been issued to all the District Collectors to take over
the management and affairs of the Primary Agricultural Credit Co-operative
Societies; which is an instance of clear malafides, showing undue haste in
dealing with the matter.
9. Learned counsel for the Petitioners also placed reliance on the law declared by
the Apex Court in the matter of State of M.P. and Others v. Sanjay Nagayach
and Others reported in AIR 2013 SC 1921 holding that an elected body cannot
be simply thrown away like this, adding that the said judgment was rendered by
the Apex Court in the light of mushrooming of several cases in different States
projecting similar cause of action.

85
10. Shri Amrito Das, the learned Additional Advocate General submits that the idea
as to the scope of applicability of the statutory provision, as understood by the
Petitioners, is not correct. It is pointed out that the Government is having every
power to frame a Scheme of reorganization in public interest under Section 16-
C of the Act, which is totally different from the power and procedure specified
under Section 16. It is stated that there is a 'non-obstante Clause' in Section
16-C, by virtue of which, the Scheme of reorganization can be formulated
notwithstanding anything contained in any Act or Rules, which hence is equally
applicable in respect of the tenure mentioned under Section 49(7-A) of the Act
as well.
11. The learned Additional Advocate General further submits that the concept and
nature of Primary Agricultural Credit Co-operative Societies is very important
and that such Societies cannot do anything contrary to the purpose for which,
they have been constituted by the State. Amongst the duties conferred upon the
said Societies, it is mainly to make available loans to the farmers, distribute
seeds and fertilizers and procure the Agricultural produce paying reasonable
price prescribed by the Government; adding that the finance provided by the
said Societies to loans is 'interest free'; thus with intent to drive out private
money lenders from the field. It is also pointed out that there was no
reorganization after 1964 and that the present scenario, which separates
different areas/ villages with regard to natural boundaries, is causing much
hardships to the farmers. Hence, an attempt was made by the erstwhile State
Government in the year 2007 to bring about reorganization, which however did
not succeed. There was a further move in the year 2013/2014 as well, which
also did not come through and the task has now been taken by the present
State Government, in the best interest of all concerned, adding that there is no
instance of any malafides. The learned counsel submits that the elected bodies
of the different Primary Agricultural Credit Co-operative Societies have come

86
into power on different dates and as such, there is no point in permitting or
waiting till completion of tenure of any particular Society to have the
reorganization implemented, as the expiry of the tenure of different Societies
will definitely fall on different dates. It is in the said circumstance, that the
Scheme has been sought to be implemented immediately and that election
would be conducted without delay, when the Petitioners who are already in
power can also contest. The said process is to be undertaken by the Election
Commission, a totally independent body, where the State Government does not
have any control or say.
12. It is pointed out that the opportunity to submit representation is to have the
matter considered by the Government and to decide whether the Scheme
requires any change or modification. This right is always there and will be
considered and finalized in due course, by virtue of the powers specifically
conferred upon the Government under sub-section (3) of Section 16 (C) of the
Act. The attempt of the Government is only to bring about the 'territorial
reorganization' and that the rights and interests of the Societies or its members
are not intended to be adversely affected in any manner; submits the learned
Additional Advocate General.
13. The learned counsel for the Petitioners placed a copy of the proceedings
bearing No. F-15-35/15-02/2019 dated 27.08.2019 issued by the Department of
the Co-operative, State of Chhattisgarh and addressed to all the District
Collectors in relation to the appointment of the “authorized officers” in the
Primary Agricultural Credit Co-operative Societies pursuant to Anenxure P/1
Reorganization Scheme. It is mentioned therein that the State Government has
decided for reorganization of all the 1333 Primary Agricultural Credit Co-operative Societies functioning in the State, which apparently is contrary to the
'preface' to Annexure P/1 Reorganization Scheme.

87
14. The above instruction further says that due to reorganization, the elected Board
of Societies will cease to function and the competent authority will appoint
authorized officers in the Societies. It is stated that in the Co-operative
Department, large number of employees are not available, and therefore, the
Government have decided also to appoint “Grade-III employees” of other
Departments than Co-operative Department i.e. Agricultural and Revenue, as
authorized officers; simultaneously giving other appropriate directions. This by
itself is an indicator that the Government has not set the field, providing
sufficient infrastructure to take over the management on reorganization and to
run the Societies, protecting the interest of members and all concerned.
15. We heard the matter extensively with regard to the prayer for interim relief on
06.09.2019 and observed that the scope and contents of the report of the
Registrar were not revealed in Annexure P/1 Reorganization Scheme;
simultaneously observing that particulations of reorganization had to be
ascertained. Insofar as there was no specific case against any particular
Society, as to any lapse/default or mismanagement, taking over the
'Management' of the Societies from the body which came into power through a
democratic process of election; that too abruptly and without any notice or
opportunity of hearing was to be deprecated. More so, when the 'Management'
and affairs of the Societies was sought to be entrusted to Grade-III employees
of Agricultural and Revenue Department as per the proceedings dated
27.08.2019; who may not have any exposure and understanding as to the
affairs of Co-operative Department or its laws. Observing that the plight of the
Societies and the members cannot be predicted, we passed an interim order of
stay with regard to the further proceedings pursuant to Annexure P/1
Reorganization Scheme dated 30.07.2019 and all other steps with regard to the
proceeding bearing No. F-15-35/15-02/2019 dated 27.08.2019 appointing
'Authorized Officers'.

88
16. The State has filed their return dated 01.10.2019 denying all the averments and
allegations raised in the writ petition and highlighting the steps taken by the
Government to reorganize the Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies
as a step for the benefit of all concerned. More so, when the field was lying
dormant for nearly six decades after the formation of the Primary Agriculture
Credit Co-operative Societies in the early 1960s, when it was part of the
undivided State of Madhya Pradesh. It is pointed out that the Primary
Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies were constituted by the State
Government at the lowest village level for a given area, depending upon the
population and territorial lie and location. This was done looking at the
geographical positioning of the village and the best connectivity between the
group of villages who are clubbed together and placed in the particular Primary
Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies.
17. The larger object was to ensure co-operation and to develop Primary Agriculture
Credit Co-operative Societies as a Co-operative institution at village level and
model 'by laws' were framed by the Department, a copy of which has been
produced as Annexure R/1. It is also pointed out that the Primary Agriculture
Credit Co-operative Societies so constituted cannot engage themselves in any
other activity, but for ensuring the welfare of the agriculturists who are directly
associated to these Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies for their
day to day activities, who in turn were functioning under the supervision of State
Government extending benefits in implementation of Governmental policies.
Considering the change came into effect after 1960, steps were taken to
reorganize the Societies by bringing about a Scheme in the year 2007, a copy
of which has been produced as Annexure R/2. Though much deliberation was
done, it could not be implemented or executed despite all efforts and hence

89
steps were initiated again by the end of 2013 and early 2014 to bring about a
new Scheme for reorganization of the Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative
Societies, as evident from Annexure R/3 and Annexure R/4.
18. Though some follow-up steps were taken in the year 2016, reorganization of
Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies was remaining as an
uncompleted task. It is stated that there is no political agenda or malice in
reorganization of the Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies as
alleged. Based on the 'sea change' of the factual situation, due to passage of
time and the various demands for reorganization from different corners (which
fact is sought to be established with reference to Annexure R/7), a detailed
report was submitted by the Registrar of Co-operative Societies on 06.07.2019,
a copy of which alongwith the Scheme was forwarded, as borne by Annexure
R/8. It is asserted that the process of reorganization was a continuing one,
which fact is sought to be substantiated by producing copies of 'note-sheets' of
the file of the Registrar as borne by Annexure R/9, adding that State
Government has shown no haste in notifying the Scheme of reorganization. It is
pointed out that Section 16-C of the Act stands on a different pedestal, being a
self-contained one and by virtue of the 'non-obstante clause', the other aspects
dealt with elsewhere in the statue including the tenure of the Board as provided
under Section 49(7-A) (i) of the Act are not attracted.
19. With regard to the averments raised by the Petitioners in the writ petition with
reference to the incorporation of various provisions in the Constitution of India
under Part IX B, including prescription of tenure under Article 243ZJ (2) by way
of the 97th Amendment to the Constitution, it is pointed out that the said
amendment has been declared unconstitutional by the High Court of Gujarat in
Rajendra N. Shah vs. Union of India reported in 2013 SCC Online Guj 2242.
It is also pointed out that, though the said verdict has been subject to be

90
challenged before the Apex Court by filing SLP No. 25266-25267 of 2013 and
the SLP is pending, no interim stay has been granted and as such, Part IX B of
the Constitution does not exist as on date.
20. Contentions are raised with regard to violation of principles of natural justice as
to denial of opportunity of hearing before issuing the Reorganization Scheme,
pointing out that the situation covered by the Section 16 of the Act providing for
reorganization by the Society itself is a matter of voluntary course, which is
different from the course under Section 16-C of the Act where it is for the
Government to bring about Reorganization Scheme, on getting satisfied of its
requirement and this is saved by virtue of the specific 'non-obstante clause'
contained therein. Section 16-C of the Act does not contemplate any show-cause notice to the members of the Societies and hence the idea of the
Petitioner is wrong and misconceived. The Petitioner has filed rejoinder, virtually
reiterating the submissions raised in the writ petition.
21. The pleadings and submissions made across the Bar clearly reveal that Section
16-C of the Act is not under challenge and the grievance is only with regard to
Clause-8 of the Scheme, whereby the Administrative Management of the
Societies concerned is sought to be taken over as temporary measure by the
Respondents, under the pretext of the Reorganization Scheme for the Primary
Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies. The term Primary Agriculture Credit
Co-operative Society defined defined under Section 2(u-ii) of the Act is as
follows:
“2(u-ii) “Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative
Society” means a society organized with the main
objective of making credit available for agriculture
production and includes Primary, Service Cooperative
Society and Adim Jati Sewa Sahakari Smiti;”

91
In fact, reorganization of a Society is possible under three different
contexts, as dealt with under two different channels i.e. under Section 16 and
Section 16-C of the Act. It will be worthwhile to extract the said provisions as
well; to have an easy understanding, as given below:
“16. Reorganization of Societies.-
(1) In this section-
(a) "affected society" means a society which
decides to reorganize itself in any of the
manners specified in sub-section (2); and
(b) "resulting society" means a society-
(i) which is formed as a result of
amalgamation under clause (a) of sub-section (2); or
(ii) to which the assets and liabilities of the
affected societies are transferred in
whole or in part under clause (b) sub-section(2); or
(iii) which is formed as a result of division
under clause (c) of sub-section (2); or
(iv) which is the result of change of class as
provided in clause (d) of sub-section (2).
(2) A society may, by resolution passed by two-thirds majority
of the members present and voting at a special general
meeting held for the purpose decide to reorganize itself
by-
(a) amalgamating itself with another society; or
(b) transferring its assets and liabilities in whole or in part to
any other society; or
(c) dividing itself into two or more societies; or
(d) converting itself into a class of society, the object of which
is materially different from that under which it has been
classified under this Act:
Provided that no such decisions shall take effect unless it
is approved by the Registrar:
Provided further than in case of a Co-operative Bank, the
Registrar shall not give his approval save with the
previous sanction in writing of the Reserve Bank;
(3) Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-section (2)
where the Registrar is satisfied that it is essential in the
public interest or in the interest of the members of the
affected societies or necessary to secure the proper
management of any society, he may direct that any
society or societies shall reorganize itself or themselves
in any one or more of the modes indicated in sub-section
(2):

92
Provided that, in the case of a Co-operative Bank, the
Registrar shall not issue any direction save with the
previous sanction in writing of the Reserve Bank:
Provided further that before a final order under this sub-section is passed by the Registrar every society
concerned shall be given an opportunity of expressing its
opinion on the reorganization proposals.
(4) The procedure for reorganization of a society in
accordance with the decision under sub-section (2) or the
direction of the Registrar under sub-section (3), as the
case may be, shall be such as may be prescribed.
(5) If any such reorganization under sub-section (2) or (3) is
likely to affect in any manner the interest of any person,
notice thereof shall be given to all such persons and
every such person shall be given an option, to be
exercised within a month from the date of the issue of
such notice, of either becoming a member of the resulting
society or societies or of demanding the payment of the
share or interest or dues in respect of the affected
society, as the case may be.
(6) No reorganization shall be final until the assent of every
such person whose interest is likely to be affected, has
been obtained or deemed to have been obtained by
virtue of his failure to exercise the option given to him,
and further, unless all the claims of persons who have
exercised the option of demanding the payment of their
shares or interests or dues under sub-section (5) have
been met in full.
(7) Every resulting society under this section shall have such
constitution, property, powers, rights, interests,
authorities, duties and obligations as may be specified in
the scheme of reorganization and every such scheme of
reorganization may contain such consequential,
incidental and supplemental provisions as may, in the
opinion of the Registrar, be necessary to give effect to
such scheme.
(8) Notwithstanding anything contained in the Transfer of
Property Act, 1882 (IV of 1882) or the Indian Registration
Act, 1908 (XVI of 1908), a resolution of a society
approved by the Registrar under sub-section (3) shall be
sufficient conveyance to vest the assets and liabilities of
each affected society in the resulting society or societies
concerned, such vesting being subject only to the
provisions of the scheme of reorganization.
(9) The reorganization of societies shall not in any manner,
whatsoever affect any right or obligation of the resulting
society or societies or render defective any legal
proceedings by or against the society or societies and
any legal proceedings that might have been continued or
commenced by or against the society or the societies, as
the case may be, before the reorganization may be
continued or commenced by or against the resulting
society or societies.

93
(10) Where any two or more societies have been amalgamated
or a society has been divided or converted, the
registration of such society or societies shall be deemed
to have been cancelled on the date of registration of the
amalgamated society or of the converted society or of the
new societies into which the society may have been
divided.
(11) Where a land mortgage bank has been reorganized by
amalgamation with a central bank, the amal gamated
bank shall in respect of all transactions relating to the
land mortgage bank business made on and after such
amalgamation be deemed to be a land mortgage bank
within the meaning of the law relating to Land Mortgage
Banks for the time being in force.
Explanation.- For the purposes of this section "Central
Bank" means a society with the words "Central Bank” or
"District Bank" as part of its name and whose main object
is to finance societies which are members of it and lie
within a specified area.
(12) Every scheme of such reorganization shall be published
in the official Gazette for general information.
16-A. xxx xxx xxx
16-B. xxx xxx xxx
16-C. Government's power to make scheme of
reorganization in the public interest.- (1)
Notwithstanding anything contained in this Act or the
Rules made thereunder, if the State Government, on
receipt of a report from the Registrar or otherwise, is
satisfied that in the public interest it is necessary to
reorganize any society or societies for the purpose of
securing proper implementation of development
programmers, the State Government, as it may deem
necessary, may make such scheme of reorganization and
issue orders to implement above scheme:
Provided that in case of a Co-operative Bank, prior
sanction in writing of the Reserve Bank shall be
necessary.
(2) There shall be the provisions in scheme of reorganization
regarding :-
(a) Method of reorganization;
(b) Procedure for reorganization;
(c) To the membership, registration, management,
assets and liabilities, powers, rights, interests, duties,
staff and conditions of employment of such society or
societies which are made after reorganization;
(d) Such other consequential, incidental and
supplementary provisions as may be necessary;
(e) Any other subject as may be deemed necessary by
the State Government.

94
(3) The State Government may modify or cancel any
reorganization scheme made or issued any orders under
sub-section (1).
(4) The provisions regarding every reorganization scheme
and the orders issued by the State Government shall be
binding upon the interested parties.
(5) Every scheme of such reorganization shall be published
in the official Gazette for general information.”
22. Section 16 of the Act clearly shows that reorganization of the Society is possible
if a resolution is taken by two third majority of the society in this regard, as
mentioned under Sub-Section (2); however, subject to the condition as given in
the proviso, that no such decision shall take effect unless approved by the
Registrar. This means, it is clearly a voluntary act to be pursued by the Society
itself, subject to approval by the Registrar. Under Sub-Section 3 of the Section
16 of the Act, notwithstanding anything contained Sub-Section (2), it is possible
for the Registrar to direct the Society to be reorganized, if he is satisfied that it
is essential in public interest or in the interest of the members of the affecting
Society or necessary to secure the proper management of any Society, subject
to the condition that, it can be invoked only after giving an opportunity of
hearing on the proposal.
23. Sub-Section (5) of Section 16 of the Act stipulates that, whether it be
reorganization under Sub-Section (2) or Sub-Section (3), notice thereof shall be
given to all the persons who are likely to be affected in any manner and option
is to be exercised within one month, either to become a member of the
proposed society or demanding payment of the share or interest or dues in the
affected Society, as the case may be. Sub-Section (6) of Section 16 of the Act
places a further rider to the effect that, no reorganization shall be final, until
assent of such person, whose interest is likely to be affected, has been
obtained.

95
24. Coming to Section 16-C of the Act, no opportunity of hearing or assent of any
person interested is envisaged and the power, which is vested upon the
Government, to reorganize the Society or Societies is notwithstanding anything
contained in the Act or the Rules. This being the position, the point to be
considered and decided is whether the said power has been validly invoked and
whether the course of action sought to be pursued as per Annexure P/1 and in
particular Clause-8 dealing with the supersession of the Administration
Management of the Society is correct and sustainable in the eye of law.
25. There is no dispute to the fact that the term of the elected Board of the Society
is to be 5 years as given under Section 49(7-A) (i) of the Act and also as
envisaged under Article 243ZL of the Constitution of India brought about as per
97th Amendment. As mentioned already, the 97th Amendment to the Constitution
having already been set aside by the Gujarat High Court as per the decision
cited by the Rajendra N. Shah (supra) and further since no interim stay has
been granted by the Apex Court where the challenge is raised against the said
verdict is pending, it cannot be said that the said provision is continuing in
operation. But the fact remains that Section 49 (7-A) (i) still stands and when
the statute stipulates the tenure of 5 years, could it be washed off by Clause-8
of Annexure P/1 scheme, is the question. Since Section 16-C of the Act confers
power upon the Government to bring about Reorganization Scheme,
notwithstanding anything contained in the Act or Rules, the situation is taken
over by the 'non-obstante clause' and if the proceeding is validly issued under
Section 16 (C) of the Act, the other provisions including the tenure prescribed
under Section 49 (7-A) (i) of the Act cannot come into play. We answer the
position in favour of the State in this regard.
26. As noted earlier, the question is whether Annexure P/1 has been validly issued
in terms of Section 16-C of the Act ? The learned counsel for the Petitioner
points out that, though the notification dated 30.07.2019 issued in this

96
connection refers to the report of the Registrar, to the effect that need was felt
by the Government that 'some' Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative
Societies were to be reorganized, steps were taken by the Respondents to
effect reorganization in respect of all the 1333 Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies in the State. The en-mass reorganization in this regard does
not tally with the projected cause for reorganization with reference to the
changes in the lie and location, topography extent and coverage of various
villages; the access/connectivity by roads, rail, bridges and such other
circumstances and the purpose of creation of Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies with reference to implementation of the developmental
activities in the State. It is also pointed out that the various
obligation/responsibilities stated as to be carried out by Primary Agriculture
Credit Co-operative Societies, as put forth by the learned Additional Advocate
General, are actually not the objects to be met by such Societies, as they are
constituted only to meet the requirements under Section 2(u-ii) and nothing
else. We find it difficult to accept the said submission completely, as the
terminology used under Section 2(u-ii) is with reference to the 'main object'
which does not leave out the incidental objects, if any. There may be such other
incidental activities in terms of the 'by laws' approved by the Registrar and
hence this cannot be a ground of attack for the Petitioners. The 'main object', as
put forth by the learned Additional Advocate General, is to make available credit
at '0% interest' to the farmers, as per the policy of the Government, by way of
short terms loans. The other objectives are to supply seeds/fertilizers to the
farmers at subsidise rate and steps with regard to the procurement of
agricultural produce from the members/farmers for a minimum support price, to
ensure that the farmers are not trapped or exploited. The version of the
Petitioners is that, as per the mandate of the Section 10 of the Act, the Societies
are classified as Consumer's Society, Farming Society, Federal Society, Central

97
Society, Housing Society, Marketing Society, Multipurpose Society, Producers'
Society, Processing Society, Resource Society, General Society and Industrial
Society and such Societies are separately defined under Section 2 of the Act.
Insofar as separate Societies are to be formed, conferring different tasks, it
cannot be the task or look out to the Primary Agricultural Credit Co-operative
Societies whose job is only to provide credit to the farmers as mentioned under
Section 2(u-ii). We do not find any merit in the said submission; for the reason
noted already.
27. With regard to the invocation of power under Section 16-C of the Act, the
learned counsel for the Petitioners highlighted the absence of requirements
under the provision, particularly with regard to the absence of recording
'satisfaction' of the Government for bringing about the Reorganization Scheme,
with reference to the 'notings' in the file of the Registrar. The learned counsel
submits that all has transpired on a single day i.e. on 28.06.2019, as revealed
from the endorsements made by the Assistant Registrar, Additional Registrar
and Registrar. It is pointed out that, though a Committee to be headed by the
Managing Director of the State Co-operative Bank, to find the ways and means
was constituted as per proceeding dated 24.12.2016, report of the Committee
was stated as not received by the State Government and hence the file was put
up for further action on 28.06.2019. Referring to the said 'note' in the file, the
Additional Registrar has endorsed that a meeting of the Managing Director of
the Apex Bank and the Joint Registrar can be convened, to meet the situation,
as per the endorsement dated the same day i.e. 28.06.2019 in terms of the
'wishes of the Government'. With reference to the said 'note', the Registrar
endorses on the same day i.e. on 28.06.2019 and orders to call for the meeting
as above on 01.07.2019, with a direction to prepare the 'agenda' and also to
call for a Draft of the Reorganization Scheme from the Managing Director of the
Apex Bank and the Joint Registrar, to be sent to the Government under Section

98
16-C of the Act. What transpired thereafter, is not known. It is all of a sudden,
that report of the Registrar comes on 06.07.2019 with the Scheme and hence
there is no 'satisfaction' of the Government, to have reorganization in terms of
Section 16-C of the Act, submits the learned counsel.
28. The learned counsel for the Petitioners submits that the hidden agenda is only
to pull down the duly elected bodies, particularly the representatives of the
Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies in the Apex Bank. The version
of the Respondents with reference to Clause-8 of the Annexure P/1 Scheme (in
relation to the three tier structure of the institution with Primary Agriculture
Credit Co-operative Societies at the bottom level, Central Societies at the
District/Block level and Apex Society at the State level) and with reference to
Annexure R/7, that requests were received by the Government from different
corners in this regard was not a sufficient ground to have ordered
reorganization in terms Section 16-C of the Act; insofar as some of such
requests made by the Gram Panchayat were only to open new Primary Co-operative Bank in their areas. Reorganization was sought for only by 12
Societies, with other requests from other corners. The so-called 12 Societies
were not having any representative capacity and if at all they wanted to have
reorganization, they could have done it in terms of Section 16(ii) of the Act
taking a voluntary course in terms thereof or could have caused it effected
under Section 16(iii) on the basis of satisfaction and the action of the Registrar.
En-mass reorganization by the Government, without proper and valid inputs
and without recording proper 'satisfaction', is not correct or sustainable in law,
submits the learned counsel; adding that Section 16-C of the Act visualizes only
exceptional circumstance.
29. The learned counsel for the Petitioner in Writ Petition (C) No. 3221 of 2019 and
Writ Petition (C) No. 3222 of 2019 submits that in a Scheme for reorganization,
there shall be provisions for various requirements, which includes

99
'management' as well. But Section 16 (2) (C) dealing with management does
not say anything with regard to 'supersession' of the elected body. In fact,
supersession is envisaged only under Section 53 of the Act and as per Section
48 of the Act, the management of the Societies always stands vested upon the
members. But, by virtue of Clause-8 of Annexure P/1 scheme, it is thrown to
wind, which is ultra vires to Section 48 and 53 of the Act. It is also added that
the attempt to cause to manage the Societies through 'Class-III employees' of
other Departments (Agriculture and Revenue) is totally alien to the scheme of
the Act. Reliance is sought to be placed on the verdict passed by the Division
Bench of Madhya Pradesh in the case of Kamta Prasad vs. Registrar, Co-operative Societies, Madhya Pradesh and Another reported in AIR 1967 MP
211 to explain the concept of co-operative movement, which is a democratic
process; besides placing reliance on the verdicts passed by the Apex Court in
the case of K. Shantharaj and Another vs. M.L. Nagaraja and Others
reported in AIR 1997 SC 2925 and in the case of Jt. Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala vs. T.A. Kuttappan and Others reported in AIR
2000 SC 2378, asserting in categorical terms that the Administrator appointed
has no power to enroll new members; thus, explaining the scope and meaning
of the word 'management', which in fact is upon the members of the Societies
and the elected body chosen by such members.
30. Shri Rajeev Shrivastava, the learned standing counsel appearing for the
Election Commission submits that the Election Commission is only a formal
party and no relief is sought for against them. It is also pointed out that the
Government/Department had placed a request to postpone the election, with
reference to the ongoing steps for reorganization of the Primary Agriculture
Credit Co-operative Societies and in the said circumstance, the election has
been postponed on 05.08.2019.

100
31. With regard to the satisfaction of the Government to invoke the power under
Section 16-C of the Act for reorganization of the Societies, it can be done either
'on the basis of the report of the Registrar' or 'if the Government is otherwise
satisfied'. During the course of hearing, the original files of the Registrar and
those of the Government were produced by the learned Additional Advocate
General. The original file of the Government starts with the 'first page entry'
dated 01.07.2019, with the endorsement made by the Hon'ble Minister for
Department of Co-operative Affairs as to necessity to reorganize the Societies
in view of the developments occurred during the past several years. But the
'notings' in the file of the Registrar (with reference to the endorsements made
on 28.06.2019 as to the meeting scheduled to be held between the Managing
Director and the Joint Registrar on 01.07.2019 or its outcome) are not
connected to the proceedings of the Hon'ble Minister, leading to the order dated
01.07.2019.
32. Despite the specific query raised by the Court and inspite of the best efforts
made by the learned Additional Advocate General, also availing the assistance
of the senior officials representing the State/Department present in the Court, it
was not satisfactorily explained. It was submitted before the Court that,
pursuant to the endorsement on 28.06.2019 in the file of the Registrar, letters
were sent to convene a meeting on 01.07.2019. Though the meeting was held
on 01.07.2019, no deliberations were made and no minutes were recorded. If
that be the position, there is a vacuum or a missing link in this regard; in the file
placed before the Hon'ble Minister who passed an order on 01.07.2019
recording 'satisfaction' of the need to have the Reorganization Scheme, giving
further directions.
33. Another major discrepancy is that, pursuant to the above order passed by the
Hon'ble Minister on 01.07.2019, as revealed from the Government file, it was
ordered to be communicated and a letter was directed to be issued to the

101
Registrar. The letter dated 05.07.2019 issued from the Ministry reached the
office of the Registrar situated nearby, only on 11.07.2019 as borne by the
endorsement/seal affixed on the original letter placed as part of the original file
of the Registrar. If the order dated 01.07.2019 of the Hon'ble Minister reached
the Registrar only on 11.07.2019, how could the Registrar call for 'Draft of the
Scheme' and forward the Scheme on '06.07.2019' (which has been referred in
the notification as the basis for having ordered reorganization), remains a
matter of mystery and remains unexplained.
34. Question is whether the challenge raised by the Petitioners against 'Clause-8' of
Annexure P/1 Scheme is having any basis and if the stand of the Respondents
in taking over the 'Management' pursuant to reorganization and entrusting it
with Class-III officers of other Departments (Agriculture and Revenue) till proper
election is conducted, could be sustained.
35. Considering the above question in the larger perspective, particularly in public
interest, the State has to satisfy the rationale for taking the decision. It remains
a fact, that the Societies Act was brought about in the year 1960 and the
Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies were formed immediately
thereafter. The object of creation of such Societies was more with intent to
provide a helping hand to the farmers for implementing the various
developmental measures brought about by the State, as a matter of policy,
including to provide credit at 0% interest. Various developments have taken
place during the past six decades, during which period, the lie, location,
topography and accessibility have undergone drastic changes. Connectivity by
different ways and means has been established by constructing roads, bridges
and such other measures. The 'need of the hour' with reference to the benefits
obtainable to the agricultural farmers is not disputed by the Petitioners. In other
words, there is no dispute for the Petitioners as to the need for reorganization
and there is no challenge against the Reorganization Scheme as such or

102
Section 16-C of the Act. This is more so, in view of the repeated assertion made
by the learned counsel for the Petitioners during the course of hearing that they
are not challenging the statutory provisions and that they are not against the
reorganization, but for the grievance against Clause-8 of the Annexure P/1
scheme providing for supersession of the Management of the elected Boards
and nothing else. There is no prayer in the writ petitions to set aside the
Reorganization Scheme or to declare the course and proceedings with
reference to Section 16-C of the Act as invalid. No specific pleadings have been
raised in this regard as well, except to interdict 'Clause-8' of Annexure P/1. In
view of the settled law that, no relief which is not claimed in the writ petition can
be granted, we do not find it necessary to go into these aspects since the
provision i.e. Section 16-C of the Act envisages the action by the Government
not only on the report of the Registrar, but also if it is satisfied otherwise.
Sequence of events referred to as to the need felt to reorganize the Societies
by the passage of time, futile steps taken in the year 2007, initiation of fresh
steps in the year 2013-14 and the various proceedings pursued in the year
2016 lead to an inference that the Government was feeling it necessary and
was satisfied to have the reorganization in terms of the Section 16-C and we
hold it accordingly.
36. As pointed out by the learned counsel for the Petitioners, the 'Management' of
the Society is always vested upon the members, as envisaged under Section
48 of the Act. The management in all respects cannot be taken over by the
Departmental Authorities except for limited extent, as it is necessary to meet the
stop gate arrangement, as made clear in the Apex Court in K. Shantharaj and
Another (supra) holding that the Administrator cannot enroll new members of
the societies. Validity of Clause-8 of Annexure P/1 Scheme has to be
considered in this context.

103
37. Clause-8 of Annexure P/1 reads as follows:
“08. izCkU/k%&

The translated version of Clause-8 as given by the learned counsel for the
parties pursuant to the order dated 02.09.2019 passed by this Court is to the
following effect:
“8. Management :-
(a) Right from the time of the Plan coming into effect,
wherever there will be an elected Board in the affected
societies and the resultant societies, the same and the
representative of such society shall cease to work and in
order to manage the business of the society any person
or persons may be appointed temporarily by the orders of
the Sub / Assistant Registrar for the period stated in the

104
order or till the election of new Board. In case of more
than one person, a person shall be appointed President
but in the event of appointing non-government person /
persons, he or they shall be among the members of the
society.
(b) The management of new societies shall be vested in
any person / persons appointed temporarily by the orders
of the officer registering the societies.
(c) Till the election of new representative, the person
appointed in accordance with aforesaid clause (a) shall
represent the affected society, resultant society and new
society but in case of persons, the representative shall be
appointed through their resolution.”
38. It is pointed out from the part of the Government that the reorganization is
'District wise' and not State wise and that the Registrar has forwarded different
Schemes for different Districts, depending upon the requirements; adding that
they have been notified on different dates in the official gazette, crux of which
has been given below:
“DETAIL OF NOTIFICATION
S.N. Date of notification District
1. 25/07/2019 1.Raipur
2.Balodapbazar
3.Gariyaband
4.mahasamund
5.Dhamtari
2. 26/07/2019 1.Rajjnandgaon
2.Kabridham
3. 30/07/2019 1.Bilaspur
4. 31/07/2019 1.Mungeli
2.Korba
3.Jangir-Champa
5. 31/07/2019 1.Bastar
2.Kondagaon
3.Narayanpur
4.Knaker
5.Dantewada
6.Sukma
7.Bijapur

105
6. 03/08/2019 1.Koriya
2.Surajpur
3.Sarguja
4.Balrampur
7. 08/08/2019 1.Raigarh
2.jashpur
8. 28/08/20149 1.Durg
2.Balod
3.Bemetara
39. It is brought to the notice of this Court, as discussed already, that after issuing
Annexure P/1 notification, another instruction bearing No. F-15-35/15-02/2019
dated 27.08.2019 was issued by the Secretary to the Chhattisgarh State
Department of Co-operative to all District Collectors appointing Grade-III
employees of other than Co-operative Department (Agriculture and Revenue)
as 'Authorized Officer' to take over the 'management' because of paucity of
sufficient Officers in the Co-operative Department. The said Circular reads as
follows:
“State of Chhattisgarh
Department of Cooperative
Mantrayalya
Mahanadi Bhawan, Nava Raipur, Atal Nagar
No./F-15-34/15-02/2019/ Atal Nagar, /2019
To,
All District Collectors,
Sub:- In relation for appointing authorized officer in Primary
Agriculture Credit Cooperative Societies.
--00--
There are 1333 Primary Agriculture Credit Cooperative
Society functioning in the State. The State Government Decided
for reorganization of the said societies for which reorganization
scheme 2019 for Primary Agriculture Credit Cooperative
Societies of All district has been issued. Due to the
reorganization the elected board of societies will cease to
function and Registrar will appoint (Deputy)/Assistant Registrar
Cooperative Societies of Distinct) as authorized officer for
functioning of societies. In the cooperative department large
number of employees were not available therefore Government
had decided also to appoint grade-III employees of other than
Cooperative department (Agriculture and Revenue) as
authorized officer.

106
The proposal by Deputy/ Assistant Registrar Cooperative
Societies of the District for appointing authorized officer from
other department will be appointed after taking approval from
you.
Hence on priority basis the above work. Should be done
so disturbance should not occur in the business of the society.
Sd/-
(Reeta Shandilya)
Secretary
Chhattisgarh State
 Department of Cooperative
No./F15-35/15-02/2019/2417 Atal Nagar, dated 27.08.2019”
40. As mentioned already, what made the Government/Department to incorporate
'Clause-8' to supersede the elected office bearers of the Societies and entrust
the 'management' with and 'Authorized Officer' that too, to the Grade-III
employees of other Departments (Agriculture/Revenue) is not explained either
in the reply or during the course of arguments; nor is it revealed from the files.
The doubt expressed by this Court in the interim order dated 06.09.2019 as to
how it can be managed by such persons, who are having absolutely no
knowledge, experience or access with the law and procedure of the Co-operative Department is not answered by the Respondents-State/Department.
There is no case for the Respondents that, any of the circumstances to
supersede the 'management' as envisaged under Section 53 of the Act or such
other contingency is brought to the notice of this Court. So also, there is no
case for them that there is any defect or lapse or mismanagement of the
Primary Agriculture Credit Co-operative Societies. The only case is that, by
virtue of the changes resulted because of the nature, lie and location of the
various villages/boundaries/roads/bridges/accessibility and the changes
occurred during the past several years, reorganization was to be effected.
Insofar as there is no objection against the reorganization, it is quite open and
possible for the Respondents to proceed with such steps pursuant to Annexure

107
P/1 Scheme, but why should there be a supersession of the elected bodies is
the only question. Since there is absolutely no explanation from the part of the
Respondents, this Court of the firm view that the elected bodies brought to
power through a democratic exercise cannot be thrown down in a casual
manner, leaving it to be managed by Class-III Government employees
belonging even to other Departments like Agriculture and Revenue, who do not
have any acquaintance with the functions, affairs or laws governing the field.
This otherwise will only promote utter chaos and will be detrimental to rights
and interest of the members/beneficiaries/farmers of such Societies. This is not
the intention of the law makers, when the provision was incorporated (Section
16-C) as part of the Act, which does not confer or envisage 'supersession', for
giving effect to the reorganization.
41. It does not mean that the Respondents have to wait till expiry of the term or
tenure of the elected bodies. The Respondents may be correct in saying that
massive reorganization is called for, where several Societies are involved
because of the circumstances which lead to the reorganization due to the
various developments taken place during the past several decades and the
eligibility of the members to be attached with the particular Society. Changes to
be effected in one Society may affect the neighbouring Society as well; thus,
involving a chain reaction, with cascading effect. The Government is justified in
pointing out that the tenure of one Society may be different from the other and
the Government cannot wait endlessly to have the tenure expired, as there
cannot be a common date to bring about the reorganization. But this does not
mean that, for effecting such reorganization, the 'management' has to be taken
over. The Government/Department is free to effect the reorganization at any
time, and based on the reorganization, it can cause the election to be
conducted; after getting the voters' list prepared in accordance with law. But this
exercise can be done by the elected body already in office, insofar as there is

108
no allegation against them with reference to any fault/lapse or any
mismanagement. As it stands so, we are of the view that Clause-8 of Annexure
P/1 Scheme providing for 'supersession' of the Societies, entrusting the
'management' to Authorized Officers of the Department and the subsequent
proceedings dated 27.08.2019, whereby Assistant Grade-III employees of the
Agricultural and Revenue Department have been appointed as Authorized
Officers do not stand the test of law and are liable to be struck off. It is ordered
accordingly.
42. In the above circumstances, while upholding the course of action sought to be
pursued by the Government for reorganization of the Petitioners-Agricultural
Societies in terms of Section 16-C of the Act, we make it clear that further steps
could be pursued by the Respondents to give effect to the same, in terms of the
Schemes as notified and subject to the procedure as mentioned therein,
however continuing to vest the 'management' of such Societies on the elected
bodies who exist as on date.
43. The original files produced before this Court during the course of hearing stand
returned to the learned Additional Advocate General.
44. We make it clear that the above discussion is only with reference to the scope of
reorganization sought to be effected under Section 16-C of the Act and as to the
sustainability of Clause-8 of Annexure P/1 Scheme. It is not with regard to
anything else.
45. The writ petitions are allowed to the said extent. No cost.
 Sd/- Sd/-
 (P. R. Ramachandra Menon) (Parth Prateem Sahu)
 Chief Justice Judge
 Brijmohan
